   1|                                                      Vera resided in Lunenburg for 45 years with her husband. She
   2|                                                      owned and operated Associated Data Concepts in Fütchburg. She
   3|                                                      had a love for antiques and her home. Her family and
   4|                                                      grandchildren were the most important things in her life.                  She
   5|                                                      wrote a column "Observation Point" for the Lunenburg Ledger
   6|                                                      Newspaper and was a member of the Lunenburg Historical
   7|                                                      Commission. Before joining her husband in running their
   8|                                                      company, she worked for many years as a secretary at the Thomas
   9|                                                      Passios School.
  10|                                                        Caroline C. /Fischer) Porter
  11|                                                      Caroline, wife of Paul, was a longtime resident of Lunenburg and
  12|                                                      co-owner of Hearthstone Realty. For many years, from 1980 to
  13|                                                      1998 she was a member of the Cemetery Commission.                            She
  14|                                                      enjoyed crocheting blankets for homeless veterans and scarves for
  15|                                                      Crocker Elementary School in Fitchburg. She loved to summer in
  16|                                                      Maine, but most of all she loved spending time with her family.
  17|                                                          Wellum George MacMumn Fe.
  18|                                                         Bill was an entrepreneur and patent-holder. After his retirement
  19|                                                         he worked as the Cemetery Superintendent in Lunenburg,
  20|                                                         courier driver and school bus driver. Bill was instrumental in
  21|                                                         the establishment of the Mossman Teen Center and was on the
  22|                                                         ground floor of the weekly newspaper, the Lunenburg Ledger.
  23|                                                         He enjoyed hunting and fishing and watching his grandchildren
  24|                                                         participate in sports, concerts and plays. Most of all, he enjoyed
  25|                                                         time with his sons.

===PAGE 10=== (ocr, 32 lines)
   1|                       • Returning the Jones House to the tax rolls with a second RFP process with the continued
   2|                           guidance of the Historical Commission
   3|                       • Moving the DPW renovation project forward with revised funding in face of increased
   4|                            material and labor costs
   5|                       • Fully enacted the Municipal Energy Aggregation Plan with Colonial Power Group with
   6|                            participating citizens enjoying reduced electric supply costs
   7|                       • Continued support for the redevelopment of the old Primary School into affordable senior
   8|                            housing with the developer working to obtain Federal Housing and Urban Development
   9|                            funding
  10|                       • Moved forward with the cost effective renovation and reorganization of Town Hall to
  11|                           improve accessibility and extend its useful life using funding from the sale of the surplus Susan Brown House.
  12|                       • Implemented use of the DPW Director's Pavement Management Plan ensuring limited
  13|                           paving dollars are spent as effectively and fairly as possible
  14|                       • Moved the Summer Street redesign and redevelopment forward with engineering firm
  15|                            VHB in conjunction with Leominster and Fitchburg so a construction ready project will
  16|                            qualify for state grant funding to improve this area aesthetically and with an eye to future
  17|                            development.
  18|                       • After a full REP process, entered into a long term contract with Casella Waste to provide
  19|                            garbage and recyeling service to town participants saving $60,000 per year in cost
  20|                       • Drained the tanks at the abandoned service station at 925 Mass Ave to avoid environmental
  21|                            damage with the costs liened to the property.
  22|                       • Signed a state wide mutual aid agreements for Public Safety and DPW allowing for shared
  23|                            resources as needed and as made available
  24|                       • After much research and study agreed to regionalize Public Safety Dispatch avoiding new
  25|                            extensive costs in state mandated staffing requirements while enabling a robust operation.
  26|                        , Dealt with another crippling storm in October which caused extensive damage to power
  27|                            system and trees requiring a significant, expensive clean up effort. Lessons learned in the
  28|                            2008 storm were enacted across Public Safety and DPW lightening the load significantly.
  29|                   2012 will be another challenging year with finances at the core or every decision and effort. As the
  30|                   town continues to work with revenue growth out of pace with national inflation, additional cuts
  31|                   and reductions will be unavoidable with the public feeling the brunt of lost services and response.
  32|                                                                           7

===PAGE 11=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                   project was a full renovation of the existing building, often referred to as "the Barn." The existing garage
   2|                   sach provictor great i proved and complant niac. deten and pactical and lightin oystems,
   3|                    existing space, also provides conference meeting space for the Boards & Committees associated with the
   4|                    Department of Public Works. If you haven't yet had a chance to tour the facility, please stop by and see it.
   5|                   The facility is spectacular. The DPW Building Committee did a fantastic job on a very small budget.
   6|                   We continue to work on a plan to completely rehabilitate the Summer Street corridor. In 2011, the Town
   7|                   approved funding for an Alternatives Analysis to determine which roadway realignment would work best
   8|                    for both residential properties and the enhancement of commercial properties.
   9|                   pursue a realignment which provides for one lane of travel in each direction, with center turn lanes at
  10|                   construction to be programmed in the area Transportation Improvement Plan [TIP]. Construction of the
  11|                   project will be fully grant funded through the TIP.
  12|                   This year, we also launched a new Town website, with the assistance of both staff and a citizens task force.
  13|                   The new website is more user friendly, both for the public and staff, and certainly more eye appealing.
  14|                   The Town will continue to make improvements to the website, by adding additional content and
  15|                   functionality.
  16|                   During 2011, the Town        entered into a 10-year contract for solid waste services with Casella Waste
  17|                   Systems, Inc. of Auburn, MA. This contract was structured in a way that citizens should see almost no
  18|                   increase in the cost of trash bags through its life. We were also able to modify the pickup schedule to a 5-
  19|                   day per week schedule, which means all trash pickup will occur earlier in the day and the trucks will be
  20|                   off the road during peak commuting times.
  21|                   Finally, the single greatest achievement in 2011 was the formation of the Nashoba Regional Emergency
  22|                   Comich Di will prich then Cobu, as a fundints arter Creatio of this sister has heraras in
  23|                   Lancaster- superior, efficient and cost-effective Emergency 911 Dispatch, in a start of the art facility.
  24|                   Creation of the district pools together the resources of all the partnering communities as well as
  25|                   substantial grant funding from the State. It is estimated that each founding member will save
  26|                   approximately $100,000 in annual operating costs, while at the same time meeting a new mandate which
  27|                   requires Emergency Medical Dispatch. If Lunenburg had to meet this mandate on its own, the increase in
  28|                   local operations would have been in excess of $100,000. This effort would not have been possible without
  29|                   the support of the Fire Chief & Department, the Police Chief & Department, the Dispatcher's Union,
  30|                   Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee.
  31|                   I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their
  32|                   time and many talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.
  33|                                                                            8

  26|                                                                      CAPITAL PLANNING
  27|                   Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY) Plan and a Capital
  28|                   Improvement Plan. These Plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
  29|                   As noted in the past reports the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis, Cost Effectiveness and
  30|                   Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six elements. Not only do they have to determine that
  31|                   the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are
  32|                   included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and
  33|                   protection of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  34|                   At the Annual Town Meeting May 2011, $538,765.00 was approved for Capital Requests. $133,765.00 was transferred from
  35|                   available funds and $405,000.00 was borrowed.
  36|                                                                          10

   4|                   under borrowing were DPW. loader, Fire. Engine 2 refit and radios, MS- Parking Lot, THMS- whiteboards, bleachers and gym
   5|                   floor, HS- Asbestos Abatement and ADA Access.
   6|                   The Committee meets each year after the Capital Budget is presented to the Town Manager and considers improvement to the
   7|                   methodology of creating the budget. In future preparation the Committee will conduct more meetings on site.

  11|                                                     PLANNING BOARD OPEN SPACE SUB-COMMITTEE
  12|                   Fiscal Year 2011 marked a very important milestone for the Open Space Sub-Committee. The Commonwealth's Executiye Office
  13|                   of Energy and Environmental Affairs Division of Conservation Services approved the revision to the Town's Open Space and
  14|                   Recreation Plan (OSRP). The OSRP gathered input and feedback from Town boards, organizations, and residents about the
  15|                   open space, conservation, and recreation needs. The Plan establishes an inventory of the resources our community already
  16|                   has, and creates an action plan for making improvements. This Plan was first drafted in 1978, and was subsequently revised and
  17|                   updated in 1985, 1990, and 1998.
  18|                   Besides being an important step in addressing our Town's open space and recreation needs, having a state-approved Open
  19|                   Space and Recreation Plan makes the Town eligible to apply for certain State grants; these grants can reimburse the Town for
  20|                   up to 62% of the cost of open space and recreation acquisitions and improvements. Historically, the Town made regular use of
  21|                   the Totate asant an advantage or touse grants since 199. The State ahased and protected erie an is to arre
  22|                   years.
  23|                   Looking forward, the Open Space Sub-Committee transitioned from a group charged with developing a plan into the Town's first- ever advisory committee responsible for guiding the implementation of the Plan. As such, the Open Space Sub-Committee also
  24|                   began consideration of the first action items outlined in the OSRP. The Plan identifies numerous improvements to our existing
  25|                   open space and recreation infrastructure, policies, and bylaws, and the action items were designed intentionally to be cost-
  26|                   neutral to the Town. The sub-committee will be working with the community and other Town boards and committees in the coming year to begin implementation.
  27|                   Marion M. Benson, Planning Director/Chair                     Robert Verge, Conservation Commission
  28|                   Richard A. Brockelman, Member At Large                        Sharon L. Jordan, Conservation Commission
  29|                   Brandon L. Kibbe, Member At Large                             Janice P. Carrier, Conservation Commission

   6|                                                           CENTRAL MASS MOSQUITO CONTROL
   7|                   The Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project (CMCP) currently provides its services to 39 cities and towns throughout Middlesex and Worcester Counties. The Project's headquarters is located at 111 Otis St., Northborough, MA. Project personnel are
   8|                   available to meet with any town board or resident to discuss the Project's procedures and activities. Our phone number is (508) 393-
   9|                   expertise, experience, and scientific research to provide our member communities with modern, environmentally sound, cost CMCP practices Integrated Mosquito Management (IMM), blending state of the art methods and techniques with
  10|                   effective mosquito control. IMM encourages the use of non-chemical means to accomplish the goal of mosquito reduction. The Mosquito Awareness program which we offer to elementary schools in our district is very popular. Project staff meets
  11|                   with students and teachers to discuss mosquito biology, mosquito habitat, and control procedures. Much of the presentation is
  12|                   videos, handouts, and coloring books help to make this an interesting program. directed towards what the children and their families can do to prevent mosquitoes from. breeding around their homes. Slides,
  13|                   cleaning clogged and overgrown waterways, mosquito breeding can be reduced, drainage areas are returned to historical As part of our effort to reduce the need for pesticides, our first line of control is our ditch maintenance program. By
  14|                   conditions, and water quality is improved. Source reduction, the elimination or reduction of a mosquito breeding source (i.e. water- holding containers), is practiced by alerting residents and business owners about potential mosquito producing sites they have
  15|                    reated. They are informed about basic mosquito biology, and the need to eliminate these man-made breeding sources. A nev
  16|                   program for CMIMCP is a source reduction program, i.e. tire recycling. Used tire casings are a preferred habitat for several species of human-biting mosquitoes, species that also carry disease. CMMCP has implemented a program to be offered in several variants to
  17|                   remove these habitats from the environment.
  18|                    eduction or wetland restoration is not feasible. We have an extensive database of known breeding sites, and we encourage the Bacterial larval control with Bti (Bacillus thuringiensis) is used to treat areas where mosquito larvae are found and source
  19|                    ublic to notify us of any areas they suspect could breed mosquitoes. Our field crews will investigate all requests and treat the are
  20|                   only if pre-determined thresholds of mosquito larvae are exceeded. Bti is a naturally occurring product, and is exclusive to mosquito larvae, preserving other aquatic organisms in their habitat.
  21|                            Our goal is to handle all mosquito problems with wetlands restoration, source reduction or larval control, but we recognize
  22|                   that there are times when adult mosquito spraying is the only viable solution. In such cases residential areas are treated with either hand-held or pick-up truck mounted sprayers. Applications are site-specific and are determined by weather, type of area and
  23|                   mosquito population levels. These applications are initiated only by request of town residents. A phone notification system has been
  24|                   installed to announce potential spray areas for member cities and towns on their scheduled evening, and this information is listed on our website.
  25|                   prescribing various control techniques. We have expanded the adult collection program to monitor for West Nile Virus in our service The project's surveillance program monitors adult mosquito and larval population density, and is the backbone for
  26|                   sampling. If a WNV or EEE hot spot is identified, surveillance is intensified to sample mosquitoes in that area and these collections area. Specialized traps are used as a mobile force for viral monitoring, and are placed in member towns on a weekly basis for routine
  27|                   are sent in to the Mass. Dept. of Public Health for virus testing.

  18|                                                                 HISTORICAL COMMISSION
  19|                  The Lunenburg Historical Commission was created to identify, safeguard and help preserve for posterity, the unique physical
  20|                  assets of the Town as exemplified by the various sites, buildings and other edifices of historic, architectural or archeological
  21|                  significance to the Town of Lunenburg
  22|                           The Mission Statement above continues to be the guiding principle for the Historical Commission to focus its efforts
  23|                  and work on a number of projects of historical consequence for the Town. The main initiatives implemented this year are as
  24|                  follows:
  25|                  Jones House - A Request for Proposal was published by the Town in anticipation of the sale of the Jones House at 42 Main
  26|                  Street as a historic property. No substantive bids were received and the Selectmen chose to re-advertise the property with some
  27|                  minor revisions to the Proposal form. Those bids were expected in early 2012 and the expectation will be to proceed with the
  28|                  sale of the Jones House as a first step in preserving this historic property and returning it to the tax rolls of the Town.
  29|                  Lunenburg Ledger - The program of periodic newspaper articles has continued to inform Town residents of the unique historical
  30|                  qualities of life in Lunenburg.
  31|                  Demolition Delay Requests - The Commission continued to work closely with the Building Inspector to review and determine the

   1|                  The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the highest
   2|                  quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   3|                  As I begin to write my final annual report as your Fire Chief, I want to remind everyone of the importance of smoke and carbon
   4|                  very early in a smoke or carbon monoxide event and will provide you time to escape from the building. Please never go to bed monoxide detectors. These detectors are required by law but more importantly, can save your life. These detectors will alarm
   5|                  knowing your detectors are not working. YOU MAY NOT WAKE UP! Should you need help to test or install your detectors, call us- we will be glad to help you.
   6|                  This year we saw Tri Town Landing and Emerald Place develop into multi-unit housing. Tri Town currently has two fully
   7|                  occupied 32-unit buildings, Emerald Place has five 24-unit buildings, five 2-unit and two 4-unit buildings. Both developments are
   8|                  fully occupied as of this report. Emerald Place continues to build two and four-unit buildings and Tri Town is projecting
   9|                  construction of one more 32-unit building. Although progressing less rapidly, we still see other building projects in the planning
  10|                  stages in town. These projects combined with our other inspections and plan reviews keep the fire prevention office very busy.
  11|                   Town meeting provided us the tunding to replace our brush truck's body (Engine 2). The1988 truck itself had very low mileage and was excellent condition. The body on the truck needed replacement to meet our current needs. The truck was sent out at the
  12|                  end of January and should be back in service the first week in March. This work will make the truck much more functional as a
  13|                  forestry unit. The new body will also be able to be removed and reused when the chassis of the truck needs to be replaced. Town meeting also provided funding for upgrading the infrastructure of the police departments radio system and replacement of some
  14|                  portable radios.
  15|                   Once again this year our department saw an increase in calls for service. As our town grows we will continue to see these
  16|                  requests increase. Our department remains committed to a combination of full and part time call firefighters and EMTs. We are
  17|                   obligated to address weekend and overnight coverage in the station for the safety of our community. Currently we staff the
  18|                  station from 6 am to 6 pm weekdays then depend on our call and career staff to respond to emergency calls after hours and on
  19|                  weekends. Although we have extremely committed and dedicated firefighters and EMT's on call, it still takes time to respond to the station, pick up the equipment and then respond to your call for assistance. When we have firefighters in the station, we can
  20|                  personnel on a per diem basis. In either case, we need to fund over night and weekend coverage for consistent response times
  21|                  24 x 7. If we were to staff the station over night and on weekends we would consider staffing with two firefighter/EMTs in order to
  22|                  enable the first medical or fire piece of equipment to respond immediately to your call, with the remainder of the call firefighters
  23|                  responding from home as the situation warrants.
  24|                           This year our incidents totaled 1780 and are as follows:
  25|                           Fires              495               Emergency Medical     808
  26|                           Inspections        417               Other                   60
  27|                  Our department continues to train for a wide variety of emergencies so we are as prepared as possible for the situations we are
  28|                  faced with. Keeping our training current is the only way we can be prepared. Even in these difficult economic times, funding
  29|                   training is not an option. The less we encounter certain emergencies, the more we need to train for them, not only for your safety
  30|                   and protection but for the firefighters' safety and survival. For example, we participated in a class regarding the new Hybrio
  31|                  technology in automobiles, in order to safely manage the hazards and extrication of victims of accidents involving hybrid
  32|                  automobiles.

   1|                 economically sound means of disposing of solid waste and wastewater"
   2|                 Public works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance
   3|                  services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the community
   4|                  through the effective delivery of many services.
   5|                 PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
   6|                  Consistent with the prioritization as determined by our Pavement Management Plan, road improvements were initiated on New
   7|                 West Townsend Rd., Lancaster Ave., and a limited section of Northfield Rd. to the Fitchburg line. The scope of the project
   8|                 included: a leveling course of asphalt followed by a top or finish course, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, drainage
   9|                 improvements and driveway aprons. As an issue of public safety, a leveling course was installed on Pleasant view Ave.
  10|                 Expanding the scope of our pavement preservation methods we added "crack sealing" to the overall program. This process
  11|                 involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement. This application can add approximately 5 years or more to the existing surface. The aforementioned Pavement Management Plan also identifies
  12|                 specific areas where this method is the best option.
  13|                  Funding continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of funding
  14|                 is the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 90 miles of paved streets is,
  15|                 by industry standards, is calculated at 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the operational budget
  16|                 remains stagnant. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of funds is necessary. A
  17|                 conservative estimate would be approximately 1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing
  18|                 allocations.
  19|                 Without additional funding our roads Town-wide will continue to deteriorate to a point where potentially the safety of our
  20|                 travelling public could be compromised.
  21|                 Line Markings
  22|                 For the first time in many years, adequate funding was made available to provide center line striping and fog lines at the curb on
  23|                 all our major roadways. Further, where applicable, crosswalks, stop bars and designated parking areas were repainted.
  24|                 FLEET MAINTENANCE
  25|                 The DPW is responsible for the fueling and repair of all Town vehicles and equipment excluding the Fire Dept. apparatus. High
  26|                 performance police units, park, cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are
  27|                 serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent. Due to the volatile global
  28|                 oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all petroleum based products continues to be a moving target.
  29|                 FACILITIES MANAGEMENI

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                     Landfill
   2|                     The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. is in compliance with the Division of Environmental Protection. Twice yearly the site is
   3|                     monitored for excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination. The perimeter fencing is
   4|                     inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
   5|                     Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be hazardous waste.
   6|                     We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP
   7|                     requires yearly testing of these materials.
   8|                     On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush,
   9|                     leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents.
  10|                     Solid Waste
  11|                     Public works orders, stores and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by
  12|                     our current vendor, Casella.
  13|                    Lake Shirley Dam
  14|                    In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of the dam
  15|                    to the Town. After completion of the reconstruction process, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping
  16|                    was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association with yearly assistance from the Lunenburg
  17|                     Fire Dept. Approximately five years ago custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of
  18|                    an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department of
  19|                    Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and future regulations is
  20|                     critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates.
  21|                    WINTER OPERATIONS
  22|                     Seasonal expenditures continue to exceed allocations resulting in deficit spending. During the winter months there is no task
  23|                    more important to public safety than clearing the roadways, sidewalks and municipal lots of ice and snow in an effort to assure
  24|                    the safe passage of pedestrian and vehicular traffic.
  25|                    As first responders, DPW personnel with the assistance of dedicated subcontractors, work tirelessly from start to finish
  26|                    regardless of the severity or duration of the event. Nowhere is our performance more critically evaluated. Plow drivers are
  27|                    required to remain vigilant and on call throughout the winter season with no additional compensation.
  28|                    PARKS
  29|                    In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners one DW employee, a Park Superintendent, oversees and maintains
  30|                    all parks and grounds exclusive of school property. Support staff and equipment is provided by public works. The new multi-
  31|                    use track complex remains under the control of the Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  32|                    CEMETERY
  33|                    One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South cemeteries, maintaining the
  34|                    grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies
  35|                    equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  36|                    As always, 1 am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow Town employees as we
  37|                    persevere through these difficult times.
  38|                    "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of Lunenburg for
  39|                    future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the
  40|                    divisions under the umbrella of public works"

  26|                                                                   SEWER COMMISSION
  27|                 The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006, with the Board of
  28|                 Selectmen serving as Sewer Commissioners during the construction and start-up of the sewer system. The Sewer Commission
  29|                 bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and implementing rules,
  30|                 regulations and policies, and through the DPW, manages and maintains the operational side of the system. Under the Town's
  31|                 Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the town manager.
  32|                 The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 70% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the
  33|                 City of Leominster, and the other 30% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. Due to increases in the rates charged by the City
  34|                of Leominster and the City of Fitchburg, the Commission again increased sewer rates by 20%, effective January 2012. The
  35|                 Commission continues to review our system for ways to reduce our expenses and improve operations to keep our costs down.
  36|                In addition, we are working to avoid major cost increases in the future by adopting policies and maintenance procedures.
  37|                                                                        24

===PAGE 28=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                  as come tell can to ta sam pams. The Commission has contracted with Weston & Sampson
   2|                  Engineering to address I/l issues and implement a flow metering program. Three new meters were installed at Massachusetts
   3|                   Avenue., Graham Street and Summer Street. Three temporary meters were also installed at other strategic locations in Town. The
   4|                  purpose of the meters was to monitor flow during high water events and help us find trouble areas that need attention. This
   5|                  testing was done in March and April and was followed up with smoke and dye testing in targeted areas. We were able to identify
   6|                  several sewer manhole construction and maintenance issues contributing to excess water entering the system, which have been
   7|                  addressed. The school department discontinued an old pipe from the Brooks House to the high school, and redirected a sump
   8|                  pump at the high school, further helping the system. However, while worthwhile, these actions only addressed a small portion
   9|                  of the excess water. Illegal sump pumps and leaking pipes leading from houses to the street are believed to be the major
  10|                  remaining issues. As a result, the Commission is communicating with residents in areas where there are known old pipes, and
  11|                  we will begin an inspection process of all homes connected to the sewer to assure that there are no extra sources of water.
  12|                  Several homes in the Whalom area, originally connected to the older Leominster sewer system, have already been required to
  13|                 connect to the Lunenburg system, discontinuing the use of a manhole and clay pipes found contributing to the 1/l. We currently
  14|                  pay the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster for sewerage that is sent to them based on water meter readings of
  15|                  properties within the sewer district. But both the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster have stated that they will
  16|                 operational costs substantially. It is for this reason that the Commission will continue to actively work to reduce excess water eventually start to charge the Town based on actual flows received into their systems, which could potentially increase our
  17|                 in the system.
  18|                  In November, we contracted for a comprehensive inspection and analysis of all pump stations. While most were in excellent
  19|                 condition, upgrades for efficiency were made and preventive maintenance needs reviewed in order to keep them running
  20|                 effectively for many more years. As part of the DPW building renovation, our sewer technician, Corey Dowd, was provided a
  21|                 work station and computer to monitor the pump stations and flow meters for up-to-the-minute reports. In addition, throughout
  22|                 the year, Corey was also able to complete the 20 training credit hours required to renew his certification as Wastewater Plant
  23|                 Operator, which was due to expire in December 2011. This certification is good for another 2 years.
  24|                 The Commission continues to review several proposed developments (Highfields, Lunenburg Village) and some developments
  25|                 presently under construction (Stone Farm, Emerald Place) to ensure that the properties meet all requirements of the sewer
  26|                 Commission bylaws and regulations. Most large projects require an engineered peer review by the Commission's engineering
  27|                 consultant which is paid for by the developer to avoid additional cost to the Town. The Commission requested and received
  28|                approval to extend the sewer district to accommodate a bisected commercial lot at 357 Electric Avenue which could not support a Title V septic system at the Special Town Meeting in December.
  29|                The Commission is performing a comprehensive review of all its governing bylaws, regulations, policies, procedures and fees to
  30|                 assure that they are consistent and fair, meet all state and federal laws and allow us to be a state of the art operation. We will be
  31|                 continually seeking ways ways to increase the number of sewer users to support our operation while protecting the
  32|                without sewers currently installed. environment. Part of this will include outreach to residents in areas listed as "to be sewered" on our sewer district map, but
  33|                The Commission continues to work on inter-municipal agreements with the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster to assure adequate capacity for the future and address cost and operational issues.

   9|                            Administrative Official. Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  10|                          To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B. The

  12|                            developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local ordinances.

  20|                  include; "setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues."

===PAGE 33=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                  Committee worked for 15 months with a sub-committee of the NMRSD School Committee, and in July 2011 at a joint meeting, a
   2|                  detailed analysis of operational costs and benefits of an expanded region projected a positive economic impact for Lunenburg,
   3|                  but not for the NMRSD, resulting in a recommendation by their sub-committee to not pursue expanding the region. Lunenburg's
   4|                 Committee and the NMRSD sub-committee worked well together and in good faith, though the process was challenging at times.
   5|                 Regionalization was one option to address the critical facility needs of Lunenburg High School. These needs were documented
   6|                 in a master plan study in 2007 as well as the recent accreditation report which placed Lunenburg High School on warning status. To quote the Commission's report,
   7|                                                   "The Lunenburg High School site and physical plant do not adequately support all aspects of
   8|                  the educational program and the support services for student learning. As a result of the age of the facility, the poor repair of
   9|                 the building, the limited technological resources, and limited space available in the building, the physical plant does not meet
  10|                 the demands of teaching and learning, and the basic expectations of a school's physical plant. These concerns dramatically
  11|                  limit the school's ability to meet the educational needs of students." The good news is that in January 2012 the Massachusetts
  12|                 School Building Authority moved Lunenburg High School into the eligibility phase to begin a partnership with the MSBA to
  13|                 identify a solution for the Lunenburg High School Facility.
  14|                 Given that three of the four schools in the district have significant facility needs, and to address MSBA concerns for the number
  15|                  of students served in a school, the School Committee voted to reconfigure the Lunenburg Public Schools from four to three
  16|                  buildings with a combined 6-12 school. A School Building Committee has been formed. We are very excited to have this
  17|                  opportunity to address the urgent needs of Lunenburg High School.
  18|                  Our schools are very fortunate to have a committed School Committee who dedicates countless hours in service to our students
  19|                 and education. These committed individuals work closely with members of other town boards and committees to ensure that
  20|                 our schools remain strong and responsive to the students we serve. We are also fortunate to have many involved parents who
  21|                  contribute in a variety of ways as classroom volunteers, in booster clubs, as Musicaiders and PTO members. The PTO, under
  22|                 the leadership of Wendy Bertrand as President again this year, is quite remarkable in the level of support it provides the students in our schools.
  23|                  Students, with the support of their teachers, continue to engage in learning about topics of concern to them through community
  24|                  service learning. This year students in Mr. Truax's Algebra math class at Lunenburg High School researched and are working to construct and maintain an ice rink for community use. Josephine Bowen represented our schools and community at Governor
  25|                  Patrick's Project 351 - 2012 day of service. Every day students across the district serve as mentors and coaches, and they
  26|                  continue to collect for the town food bank giving back to the community that has supported them and their education. The
  27|                  difficult financial times linger for many Lunenburg families, but through the efforts of our students and generosity of the
  28|                  community-at-large, help is provided. We are very proud of the students of our schools!
  29|                  This past year our schools bid farewell to two retirees who devoted many years to public service-their total tenure in
  30|                  Lunenburg represents 26 years of service. Retiring this past year were Scott Curtis, LHS tutor (13 years), and Judith Schenck,
  31|                  Grade 3 teacher (13 years). We deeply appreciate the many contributions of these dedicated and committed professionals and extend our best wishes to them in their retirement. This past year we bid farewell to Mike Barney when he accepted another
  32|                  position. I want to thank Mr. Barney for his 13 years of service. I also want to extend my appreciation to Mr. Joseph P. Dillon for
  33|                  serving our students as interim principal at LS for the past year. Lunenburg is most fortunate to have such committed and
  34|                  caring educators, faculty and administrators.

   1|                Elementary School roofs, to make improvements to the School Department's compliance with the American Disabilities Act
   2|                (ADA), to upgrade security and to make improvements to energy efficiency.
   3|                At Lunenburg High School, the front lobby area was renovated to create a secure entry; modifications continue to develop the e abricating raelongs te
   4|                front entry into an accessible ingress. Students from Montachusett Regional Vocational High School are fabricating railings to be installed in the spring of 2012 to complete a ramp from the sidewalk in front of the school to the main entry.
   5|                A 102 Kilowatt solar photovoltaic system was completed at the Turkey Hill Middle School through the Energy Efficiency
   6|                Conservation Block Grant Program using a power purchasing agreement with Broadway Electric, Inc. In June 2011 the system
   7|                located on the classroom wing began energy production and will generate approximately 114,000 KWH a year (roughly i/3 of the
   8|                school's electricity consumption).
   9|               In February 2011 Unitil funded an energy audit of the Passios Elementary School. A lighting upgrade project was developed from
  10|               the audit using $13,973 in energy savings from the FY11 School Department operating budget and $32,627 in rebate funds from
  11|                Unitil. The project was completed in August 2011 with the conversion of classrooms to 25 W T8 lamps and occupancy sensors.
  12|                Gym lighting was replaced with T5 high bay fixtures and exterior lights were converted to LED based lighting. The total project
  13|                cost was $46,660 with an estimated payback in 2.8 years based upon an estimated $5,200 savings
  14|                The Town FY12 capital program included asbestos repairs at Lunenburg High School and Passios Elementary School, front
  15|                entry modification at Lunenburg High School, re-paving of the Turkey Hill Middle School parking lot with the addition of
  16|                sidewalks to the track and to the classroom wing exits, and conversion of the blackboards to whiteboards at the Middle School.
  17|                                                          INSTRUCTIONAL SERVICES OFFICE
  18|                                                                   Karen Martin, Director
  19|                During this past year, the Instructional Services Office continued to focus on three areas critical to the District's mission: (a)
  20|                curriculum and assessment development, (b) continuous improvement for ALL and (c) meeting the academic, social and
  21|                emotional needs of our students. Following is a summary of activities and results in each of these areas.
  22|                Curriculum and Assessment
  23|                This school year the Math Curriculum Taskforce developed the K-8 Math Assessments/Understanding by Design units. The 9th-
  24|                12th curriculum is being developed in curriculum map format by individual course and aligned to the Common Core. Throughout the school year, each building has been involved in developing and refining common assessments, Understanding by Design
  25|                units and curriculum ma. These tools will ensure students have common learning experiences and that there is regular
  26|                monitoring of student performance against a standard. The Lunenburg High School has integrated school-wide rubrics to
  27|                measure student learning expectations. During the 2011-12 school year, an English Language Arts Taskforce will develop a
  28|                curriculum aligned with the Massachusetts Curriculum Framework for English Language Arts and Literacy which incorporates
  29|                Subjects. The new framework includes literacies for science and social studies.
  30|                Our district uses our MCAS (Massachusetts Comprehensive Assessment System), our AYP (Adequate Yearly Progress) and the
  31|                new Growth Model data to monitor overall performance and to provide specific feedback for teachers, students and our program. Analysis of the test data is done at the district, building, teacher and student level. We can draw some conclusions that will help
  32|                us move forward and improve instruction for all students. Key findings:
  33|                     ** Lunenburg Primary School met AYP and is on target to meet NCLB goals.
  34|                                                                        31

===PAGE 35=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                     CONTINUOUS IMPROVEMENT FOR ALL
   2|                     The No Child Left Behind legislation provides an opportunity to carefully examine performance results for all students. This data
   3|                     helps guide the school councils, administrative team and Results teams to plan for appropriate interventions, programming and
   4|                     professional development.
   5|                     School structures and collaboration focused on student learning ensure all students improve. The Lunenburg Public Schools
   6|                     has Results Teams, Understanding by Design training, Pyramid of Success/Response to Intervention, a comprehensive
   7|                     professional development program and new teacher induction program to support student learning.
   8|                     Results Teams meet one time per month during faculty meeting time. The teams use data to set goals, plan teaching strategies
   9|                     and assess the results.  This process ensures a regular review of student performance and effective teaching strategies.
  10|                     Through ongoing analysis of student results, refinement of units and increased awareness of effective assessments and
  11|                     instructional practices, student pertormance will improve. The UBD units provide additional measures in non-MCAS subjects
  12|                     ana provide additional curriculum materials for new teachers. Community Service Learning projects are being developed to
  13|                     provide opportunities for the transfer of understandings to an authentic problem and need. The district received a number of
  14|                     CSL grants and presented at the state-wide conference on CSL with a presentation on start-up ideas for districts.
  15|                     Ongoing and sustained professional development continues in a range of areas. This year graduate level courses were offered
  16|                     in Building inclusive Practices, Healthy Update, Google and Teaching Like A Champion. Our new teacher induction program
  17|                     provides ongoing support for new teachers to the district. New teachers participate in a one-day orientation, regular meetings
  18|                     during the school year, and professional development for new teachers. A mentor team supported our new teachers within each
  19|                     of the buildings.
  20|                     GEMS - Academic, Social and Emotional Learning
  21|                     This year is the sixth year of implementation of the district-wide GEMS curriculum. The vision of GEMS is ensuring all students
  22|                     grow to their potential, develop empathy towards others, value the importance of self-discipline and become contributing
  23|                     members of the community. This year, all schools are working to establish school-wide routines and structures that support
  24|                     academic, social and emotional learning for ALL children. Teachers have been involved in professional development and
  25|                     Responsive Classroom training. On professional development days, nurses, counselors, and special educators have studied the
  26|                     needs of students with disabilities and are developing a Response to Intervention protocol.
  27|                    The Instructional Services Office has a strong partnership with the individual schools, the administrative team, faculty and
  28|                    community. The combined and integrated efforts of all parties ensure continued focus on improving student performance and
  29|                    strive to provide teachers, administrators, students and the community with the necessary data, educational research,
  30|                     professional development, support and resources needed to improve student performance.
  31|                     For additional information on any of these topics, please contact Karen Martin, Instructional Services Director, at 978-582-4122
  32|                    ext. 3112 or e-mail kmartin@lunenburgonline.com.

  31|                                                            LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
  32|                        The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality early childhood education for all
  33|               students. We are a community of teachers, students, and parents working together to create a safe and respectful environment
  34|               for children. Our current knowledge of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and provides the foundation
  35|               for student learning. This vision for our students keeps us focused on the goals and objectives of the school.
  36|                        We started the 2011-2012 school year with 345 students in 15 classrooms for an average class size of 23. Our two
  37|                Preschool classrooms have a combined total of 37 students. We persistently strive to find better ways to meet the collective, as
  38|                well as the individual needs of our students. We piloted a new handwriting program in our five full-day Kindergarten classrooms
  39|                called Handwriting Without Tears. Early results are quite positive! Our Professional Development focused on differentiating our
  40|               Math Curriculum and Response to Intervention strategies. We refined our "best practices" in order to meet the needs of ALL
  41|               learners. We worked with experts in the field to provide our staff with opportunities to be "coached" in Mathematics, Literacy,
  42|               and Inclusive Practices. Teachers were trained to use a new student information management system called PowerSchool. Staff
  43|                                                                        33

===PAGE 37=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                           ve capa cao pra, mics, ana supportive or each or our members. we nave
   2|                    high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where members feel a
   3|                    sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment which is safe, promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
   4|                    field trips into the curriculum. Students have attended live performances and visited museums and other cultural venues. In Successful fundraising, with the support of the Parent/Teacher organization, has allowed us to integrate a number of
   5|                    addition, we have experienced performances in the areas of Performing Arts, Native American culture and Animal Habitats. The
   6|                    Scholastic Books fundraising program has allowed us to purchase many books and other resource materials in order to support
   7|                   our Literacy program throughout the school. We also maintained partnerships with businesses that support education such as
   8|                    Hannaford Helps Schools, Target, Terra Cycle, Box Tops for Education (General Mills), and Campbell Soup. Our recycle efforts
   9|                    this school year included the elimination of plastic utensils in the cafeteria, as well as reducing the use of Styrofoam. We
  10|                    continue to recycle foil pouches and collected over 12,000 during the 2010-2011 school year.
  11|                            Student Success Night was held for all Kindergarten, Grade 1 and 2 students, and their families in the spring of 2012.

  14|                                                          T.C. PASSIOS ELEMENTARY SCHOOL
  15|                                                                     Sara Lane, Principal
  16|                 The 2011-2012 Thomas C. Passios Elementary School year began with 358 students in 15 classrooms for an average class size of approximately 24 students.
  17|                        The school year began with great news. TCP made Adequate Yearly Progress (AYP) in student growth in the aggregate
  18|                 as well as in all of the subgroups and was designated a Commendation School by the Massachusetts Department of Elementary
  19|                 and Secondary Education (DESE).
  20|                         The wonderful achievement of the students was a testament to all of the hard work that had been undertaken
  21|                 throughout the school year by all constituencies in the school community to find better ways to meet the collective as well as
  22|                individual needs of all of our students. Monumental student growth and improvement was attributed to many different initiatives
  23|                that contributed to the success of the students including the establishment of a school mantra: "Good Enough Is NOT Good
  24|                 Enough; Strive for Excellence" - as well as the launch of the Trifecta, a three-prong program that was designed to support
  25|                 traditional classroom teaching by tailoring ancillary programs to meet the dynamic learning needs of all students.
  26|                         While all of the staff, parents, and students of TCP celebrated and were credited in making AYP, the celebration was
  27|                tempered with the knowledge that regardless of the positive inroads achieved by the students, the school is not out of the woods yet. No Child Left Behind mandates that a school must continue to demonstrate growth for two years in a row to rid itself
  28|                 of the needs improvement and corrective action status. Furthermore, the deliberate commitment to continuous improvement
  29|                cannot diminish because despite making significant strides in growth and gains in achievement, the school's performance
  30|                targets were not met. Rather, the TCP MCAS scores demonstrate that the work has just begun and that everyone needs to stay the course.
  31|                        Improved MCAS scores at TCP demonstrate that education is a journey and like any worthwhile endeavor there are
  32|                always obstacles in the road to success that must be surmounted. Change is difficult and takes time and commitment as well as
  33|                effort. For something to work, every member of the school community must be dedicated to working together to make it work
  34|                100 percent of the time. Every decision, every action, every result must be aligned with a shared vision that is consistent and
  35|                driven by the pledge to do the right things for students. High expectations must be made clear and individuals must buy into the
  36|                understanding that success is predicated upon everyone contributing. People must be given the knowledge, resources, and time
  37|                to accomplish meaningful goals. All constituencies must be cognizant of the path as well as the destination.
  38|                         Accordingly, during the 2011-12 school year, programs like the Trifecta were tweaked and enhanced by a longer school
  39|                day, a modified 45-minute block schedule that supported common planning time, a revitalized Power Block that was more data
  40|                driven, and the development and deployment of a Research Skills class using the Independent Investigation Method (IMM) that
  41|                supports the growth mindset as well as the tenants of the School-wide Enrichment Model (SEM). The staff is also using Teach
  42|                Like a Champion by Doug Lemov as an anchor book to examine best practices and share ideas at staff and common planning
  43|                                                                         35

===PAGE 39=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                     enrichment that support the academic and social-emotional success of all students.
   2|                             Over the 2011 summer, staff participated in enhanced Response to Intervention (RTI training) and throughout the
   3|                    school year staff members had opportunities to work together to build inclusive practices. RTI initiatives and consultations at
   4|                     TCP have been expanded and resulted in the establishment of a Teacher Support Team (TST) that meets twice a week to assist
   5|                     teachers in dealing with the learning and behavioral issues of their students. Additionally, teachers are building an RTI Survival
   6|                     Kit that contains examples of strategies and tactics that may be used to address dynamic learning needs.
   7|                             From July 5 - August 11, 2011, a Project-Based Learning Summer Adventures program was implemented featuring
   8|                    three two-week sessions of classes that ran four days a week for two hours each day from 9-11Am. The summer classes cost
   9|                     $75.00 and offerings during the different sessions included Quidditch, Environmental Explorers, Techno Team, Art, Drama,
  10|                    Website Design, Babysitting and Childcare, Cake Decorating, Drama, and Mad Science. All of the TCP PBL classes were
  11|                    Interdisciplinary, heterogeneous, and opened to all students entering or leaving grades 3, 4, and 5. Students who could not
  12|                    attora the fees were given scholarships. The activities were purposefully designed to be linked to life and encouraged authentic
  13|                    real-world connections.
  14|                    In the 2011-2012 school year, the TCP after school Project-Based Learning classes continued. During the first session, classes
  15|                    were offered in Writing Comic Books, Babysitting and First Aid, Cooking, Cupcake Decorating, and Mad Science. During the
  16|                     econa session, classes were offered in Homework Club, Power Up - Power Down, Music Movement, Clue Games, Mad Scienc
  17|                     ind Arts and Cratts. To offset the costs of materials and personnel, students paid a $20.00 program fee, which was waived i
  18|                    needed, and were provided with additional day care after the one-hour classes, until 5:30 PM at no additional cost.
  19|                    The PBL program is grounded in the use of in-depth, high-interest, and rigorous projects to facilitate learning, attain practical
  20|                    knowledge, and provide academic enrichment. PBL focuses on student-centered inquiry to respond to a complex issue
  21|                    problem, or challenge that links learning to real life. The PBL initiative allows students to work in groups or individually and
  22|                    encourages them to come up with creative ideas or realistic solutions and make presentations on their findings. All group
  23|                   learning is self-directed with the teacher acting as a facilitator. All of the PBL classes are infused with opportunities for research,
  24|                   reading, writing, and creative problem solving. The PBL programs have a culminating activity which is held during the last week. Parents were invited to attend demonstrations.
  25|                    In addition to PBL classes, the use of Study Island, an instructional and diagnostic web-based resource, was continued. The
  26|                   secure 24/7 Study Island website is accessed with individualized IDs and passwords and provides students with lessons in both
  27|                   the math and ELA content areas that assist in the procurement and mastery of content. A state-of-the-art, multimodal resource,
  28|                   Study Island supported the after-school and/or at home use of technology to enhance the acquisition of skills as well as develop
  29|                   proficiencies assessed on standardized tests. Teachers also used the Study Island program to build formative assessments that
  30|                   were utilized to determine the pre- and post-mastery of skills. Additionally, a Study Island facilitator was responsible for
  31|                   generating user reports, monitoring access, working with teachers to infuse lessons into content areas, and troubleshooting problems.
  32|                   The final component of the TCP Trifecta was the continuation of the Power Blocks that homogeneously grouped students based
  33|                   upon their needs, an examination of data from formative and summative assessments, and teacher input and recommendations.
  34|                   During Power Blocks, students are divided into small groups based upon their needs and abilities and each group was provided
  35|                   With strategies to help improve their achievement, increase their stamina, and acquire the test-taking techniques that will make
  36|                   them more successful. Student progress was continually assessed and monitored through the use of standardized tests, practice tests, and teacher feedback.
  37|                   By design, the Power Block class time was divided into reading, writing, and math. All students are given warm-up activities,
  38|                   short stand-alone lessons with lots of practice, modeling, and quick assessments to insure that learning occurred through the
  39|                   Creating, nola ning, an eras serie of knowing he. Buiver and mizes tea ors, lusts und aid us to prost pricected, enrichment, and linking learning to real life.
  40|                   Additionally, work continued to empower students to assume more responsibilities by expanding the Principal's Council,
  41|                   improving recycling, initiating the Read Every Day (R.E.D.) program, establishing learning contests, scheduling school spirit days, and creating staff appreciation events.
  42|                                                                            36

   5|                 The goal of all the new and time-tested programs and activities is to continue to support all of our students in attaining the skills
   6|                 that they need to become successful, contributing citizens in the global economy. We wish to further enable our students to
   7|                 collaborate, take risks, expand knowledge bases, and incorporate new tactics, technologies, and strategies into the learning
   8|                 process. Our belief is that students' skills need to be continually monitored, affirmed, reinforced, linked to life, and practiced in
   9|                  meaningful ways that promote achievement and success in and out of school.

   5|                 In July 2011, LHS Principal Mike Barney took a similar position at Mass Academy in Worcester and was replaced by
   6|                 Interim Principal Joseph Dillon for the 2011-2012 school year. During the first week of school, orientation meetings
   7|                  were held for all grades with an emphasis on the state's new bullying legislation and what the expectations were for all
   8|                  LHS students. In early September, a gth grade orientation night was held with guest speaker Julie Lesure from the
   9|                  Worcester County District Attorney's office who discussed issues such as responsible Internet use, Facebook, cyber-
  10|                  bullying and Internet safety. The evening was capped off with a question and answer forum led by four LHS students.
  11|                 NEASC ACCREDITATION
  12|                  On December 1, 2011, LHS submitted a Special Progress Report to the New England Association of Schools and
  13|                  Colleges (NEASC) with an emphasis on the standards of Curriculum and Community Resources for Learning. In the
  14|                  area of curriculum, all LHS content areas identified the primary and secondary academic, social, and civic expectations
  15|                 for which they would be responsible to ensure that each student at LHS would have regular opportunities to practice
  16|                 and achieve these expectations. Additionally, a curriculum template was developed for all curricula which included the
  17|                 following; units of study with enduring understandings, essential questions, concepts and skill, the school's learning
  18|                 expectations, and instructional strategies that include the use of school-wide analytic and course specific rubrics.
  19|                 The report also included technology upgrades that took place in 2011-2012. These upgrades included:
  20|                               Fifteen multimedia carts were purchased for LHS. Each cart is equipped with a laptop, projector,
  21|                               document camera and speakers. An additional fifteen carts are requested as part of the FY 13 Capital
  22|                               Plan.
  23|                          • Created two virtual servers, eliminating the need for multiple servers.
  24|                          • Completed work to provide wireless LAN throughout the school.
  25|                          • Updated all computers to MS Office 2010.
  26|                          • Rewired many of the high school rooms.
  27|                          • Replaced computers in the following labs: Rooms 103, 105, 204 and the library.
  28|                          • Purchased IPads for administrative staff.
  29|                          • The district contracted with PowerSchool as the new student management system for the Lunenburg
  30|                               School District.
  31|                      Regarding the Community Resources for Learning standard, the report focused on the shift from a regionalization
  32|                      discussion to a reconfiguration study. At the July 11, 2011, Lunenburg School Committee meeting, a
  33|                      recommendation was made by the School Committee Chair to form an advisory committee to explore
  34|                      reconfiguration as an option to address facility concerns at Lunenburg High School.
  35|                      A six-member advisory committee for district reconfiguration was formed in September of 2011. The charge of the
  36|                      committee was to identify the long-term educational opportunities and challenges around the reconfiguration, to
  37|                      identify organizational and facility challenges, and to articulate the educational opportunities and communicate to
  38|                      the public. In the October 19, 2011, draft of the advisory committee's findings and recommendations, the
  39|                      committee supported reconfiguration to a new 6-12 facility. In February of 2012, the Lunenburg School Committee
  40|                                                                           40

   1|                                      ...      ...
   2|                 Compiling information for the 2011 Annual Report has been an exercise of great value for me. As a new administrator,
   3|                 it has provided me with an opportunity to reflect on the achievements and activities of our students, faculty and staff. It
   4|                 has afforded me the opportunity to visit with thoughtful partners in vocational education, and has given me great
   5|                 insight into the achievements of the 2010-2011 academic year. Some of the more notable highlights include:
   6|                 • 100% of the Class of 2011 successfully passed the state-required MCAS exam in English, Math and Biology.
   7|                 • The Class of 2011 earned a 93% placement rate: 62% college acceptance or advanced technical training, 13%
   8|                     career bound in field, 9% career bound out of field, and 7% military.
   9|                 • Seventy-eight members (25%) of the Class of 2011 were awarded the John and Abigail Adams Scholarship for
  10|                     outstanding performance on the MCAS examination.
  11|                 • 100% of Dental Assisting graduates passed the Dental Assisting National Board exams in both Radiation Health
  12|                     and Safety and Infection Control.
  13|                 • 2011 graduate Alicia McKean was the first student in the school's history to be named a National Achievement
  14|                     Scholar, and was the first to receive the coveted Presidential Scholar Award, providing a "full-ride" to attend
  15|                     Boston College.
  16|                 • Eleven students advanced through local,     district and state competitions to earn the right to compete at the
  17|                     National SkillsUSA Conference in Kansas City, Missouri. The Monty Tech delegate was awarded four silver medals
  18|                     and one bronze medal.
  19|                 • Victoria Holbert was elected to the office of National SkillsUSA President.
  20|                 • Two cadets in the Class of 2011 were awarded Naval Reserve Officers Training Corps Scholarships - a first for the
  21|                     Monty Tech MJROTC unit and the school. Selected students were awarded full tuition, fees, books, uniforms,
  22|                     summer experiences, as well as monthly stipends.
  23|                     Monty Tech was selected as one of only sixteen schools in the country and the only vocational school in the
  24|                     nation to have a student science experiment launch into space aboard the last shuttle flight of the U.S. Space
  25|                     Program, STS-135.
  26|                 Much like districts across the state, Monty Tech has been affected by the nationwide economic recession. Maintaining
  27|                 high standards of fiscal responsibility, with an eye on our over-arching mission of increasing student achievement and
  28|                 understanding, this school has been able to maintain our high standards of academic success, while presenting a
  29|                 budget that is fiscally conservative. Our FY11 Annual Budget was unanimously accepted by each of the eighteen
  30|                 member communities, and reflected less than a 1% increase over the 2009-2010 Educational Plan. We recognize that
  31|                 we are in the midst of some of the most difficult economic times faced by public education and, as a result, we will
  32|                 continue to strive to seek creative ways to reduce member town assessments.
  33|                 Thank you for your interest in our 2011 Annual Report, your continued support of vocational education in North Central
  34|                 Massachusetts, and for providing our students - your students - with the resources that enable them to become
  35|                 successful, contributing members of our communities.
  36|                                                                    Our Mission
  37|                 Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocationall Technical School with the skills, knowledge, and
  38|                 abilities to bel a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
