   1|                   Superintendent-Director during the summer and began work on July 18.
   2|                   Katy Whitaker was promoted to the position of Development Coordinator.
   3|                                                                       Enrollment
   4|                   Monty Tech's October 1, 2010, enrollment included 1,400 students in grades nine through twelve. Students are
   5|                   represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (46), Ashby (42), Athol (122), Barre (37), Fitchburg
   6|                   (393), Gardner (193), Harvard (3), Holden (46), Hubbardston (53), Lunenburg (63), Petersham (6), Phillipston (20),
   7|                   Princeton (14), Royalston (21), Sterling (53), Templeton (104), Westminster (57), and Winchendon (115). The remaining
   8|                   twelve students were from out-of-district towns, including Clinton, Lancaster, Leominster, Orange and Townsend.
   9|                   The Guidance/Admissions Department received 736 applications for admission to the school. Of those, 636 were from
  10|                   students hoping to enter our incoming ninth-grade class. The balance of the applications came from students hoping
  11|                   to enter the school as upperclassmen. There were only a limited number of slots for students applying in either
  12|                   category. We admitted 382 freshmen. We admitted 28 students to the tenth- and eleventh-grade classes.
  13|                   Monty Tech continued to offer a variety of opportunities for students, parents and community members to learn about
  14|                   and visit the school. In October 2010, 1,110 district eighth graders participated in Tour Days. Students toured many of
  15|                   our twenty vocational/technical areas and learned about the challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and
  16|                   extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return at night, with
  17|                   their family members, to explore the facilities and talk with staff members. Career Awareness Night, as well as last
  18|                   spring's Open House, was attended by an estimated one thousand individuals per event, comprised of students,
  19|                   parents, and community members from throughout the district.
  20|                  The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) continued to attract large numbers of seventh and eighth grade students
  21|                   during the 2010-2011 school year. Due to the high enrollment, a third five-week session was implemented and this is
  22|                   our second year doing all three sessions. Participants had opportunities to attend hands-on after-school sessions in a
  23|                   variety of vocational/technical areas. A satisfaction survey rated this program 4.75 out of 5. Enrollment was 725,
  24|                   among the highest ever.
  25|                                                                 Class of 2011 Awards
  26|                   Members of the Class of 2011 were awarded at least $52,225 in scholarships. The Monty Tech Foundation provided
  27|                  $21,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $100 to $1,500. The Foundation also awarded
  28|                  $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals,
  29|                  continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of donations. The School
  30|                  Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
  31|                  Articulation Agreements with local colleges continue to play an important role in helping our graduates enter college
  32|                  through credits earned at Monty Tech. Approximately 65% of our graduates entered a two-or four-year college. This
  33|                   number has continued to grow over the years.
  34|                  Numerous challenges were faced during the development of the fiscal year 2010-2011 budget including rising health
  35|                  insurance, student transportation, and utility costs. Considerable effort was put forth by the School Committee,
  36|                  administration and staff to develop a cost-effective budget. The final fiscal year 2010-2011 Educational Plan totaled
  37|                  $22,225,324, which represents less than a 1% increase over the 2009-2010 Educational Plan.
  38|                  The District was audited in August 2011 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath
  39|                  and Co. from Greenfield, MA and an "excellent" report is anticipated.

   6|                     Auto Body: Collision Repair Technology: During 2010-2011, the program had eight students participate in SkillsUSA, all

  18|                     to be compatible with the new electronic steering systems and wireless communication. (Total enrollment: 63; 54

  25|               During the 2010-2011 school year, the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided special
  26|               education services to over two hundred students. In September 2010, the Student Support Services Department was
  27|               Individual Education Programs (IEP's). actively providing services to approximately three hundred students identified with disabilities, both on 504 plans and
  28|               The Student Support Services Department at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School provides support to all students. It has full-time nursing staff that administer medications, perform state mandated health screening exams,
  29|               and provide, when necessary, health information to the TEAM for a student's IEP meeting. The department employs a
  30|               full-time school social worker that is able to participate in TEAM meetings and assist students who have needs
  31|               concerning finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. Our
  32|               students have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor, part-time school psychologist and school
  33|               social worker. All of these individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency
  34|               treatment, as well as crisis intervention. We have on staff a full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all
  35|                students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three-year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time
  36|                speech pathologist who is available to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and consult with
  37|                teachers.

   3|                        • 75 Cadets attended
   4|                        • Visit to National WWII Museum, New Orleans
   5|                            Visit to NASA
   6|                • Conducted Memorial Day observation in five sending communities
   7|                • Two Cadets awarded NROTC scholarships- a first for our unit!
   8|                • Unit purchased a Marksmanship training simulator
   9|                • Conducted over 4000 hours of community service
  10|                On September 9, 2011 the Commanding General named Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School a Naval
  11|                Honor School for the 2010-2011 school year. This distinction is bestowed upon our MCJROT program in recognition
  12|                of exemplary performance over the past 12 months.
  13|                                                              Women in Technology
  14|                The ninth year of the North Central Massachusetts Women in Technology (WIT) program was a transition period for the
  15|                program. Due to company downsizing, a number of employees at the Westminster site of the program's sponsors-.
  16|                SimplexGrinnell and Tyco Safety Products-- were laid off or relocated. This resulted in fewer hands-on projects for the
  17|                students to work on and in larger groups for those projects that did run. In addition, the program was expanded to
  18|                include two additional schools: Oakmont Regional and Quabbin Regional high schools.
  19|                Technology majors. All students said they enjoyed the experience, felt they learned a lot and wanted to participate the
  20|                following year.
  21|                Through the program, qualified females have the opportunity to acquire hands-on experience, working on real world
  22|                business and industry projects, under the mentorship of company personnel. The students spend two days a month at
  23|                the industry site, where they get firsthand experience working in a corporate setting. Students interested in
  24|                participating in the program must first pass a pre-screening process at their sending schools, and then attend an all-
  25|                day Orientation Program, where they are interviewed by company personnel. Not all candidates are accepted.
  26|                A final projects presentation is held each year in May to showcase the students' completed projects for company
  27|                executives, school officials, parents and classmates. This event, and an all-day rehearsal, are held each year in Monty
  28|                Tech's Performing Arts Center.
  29|                The Greater Gardner Chamber of Commerce, serving as fiscal agent for the program, provides transportation to the
  30|               industry site through a private-sector grant.
  31|                                                               Student Athletics
  32|                The Monty Tech Athletic program enjoyed success again in the 2010-2011 school year, and we continue to see a rise in
  33|                participation each year. Eighteen teams competed in the Fall of 2010, twelve in the Winter of 2010-2011 and nine in the
  34|               Spring of 2011.
  35|               Last Fall, the Varsity Football team won only two games, but they improved every week and gave their all during
  36|               practice and games. They finished the year at 2-9 and 1-5 in Division 3A. The Varsity Boys Soccer team finished at 13-
  37|               3-2, qualifying for the post-season tournament, where they lost to Groton-Dunstable, 6-1, at the new Leominster turf
  38|               field. The JV Boys Soccer team was 5-8-2. The Varsity Golf team was 10-8, playing in the Central Mass Division 3
  39|               Tournament, where they finished 7th, They placed 5th in the State Vocational Tournament and 3id in the Colonial Athletic
  40|                                                                        51

===PAGE 55=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                      were 9-5 and showed vast improvement over the year. The Wrestling team participated in many dual meets and
   2|                     tournaments finishing at 2-13. The Co-Op Ice Hockey team did very well again as they won the Central Mass Coughlan
   3|                      Conference and participated in the District Tournament, losing in the Finals to Groton-Dunstable, 2-1. The JV Ice
   4|                     Hockey team played very well as the younger players look to improve their skills. We participated in a Co-Op Swim
   5|                     Team with Leominster and North Middlesex and again, it went very well. They swam at the Fitchburg State University pool, one of the best in the area.
   6|                     In the spring, the Varsity Softball team qualified for the Central Mass Tournament with a 10-10 record, losing to West
   7|                     Boylston, 15-2. The JV Softball team was 1-8. The Varsity Boys Volleyball team was 4-13 and the new JV Boys
   8|                     Volleyball team was 4-12. The Varsity Baseball team finished at 9-11. The JV Baseball team was 7-6 and the Freshman
   9|                     Baseball team was 1-5. The Boys Track & Field team was 10-2, 2nd in the CAL with a 5-1 record. The Girls Track & Field team was 8-4 and 3-3 in the CAL.
  10|                     The Outstanding Male and Female athletes for the 2010-2011 were Kevin Duong and Kelsie Twombly.
  11|                                                            Postgraduate and Continuing Studies
  12|                     The Postgraduate and Continuing Studies Program, also known as Monty Tech Nites, continues to update and add
  13|                     educational experiences. courses that emphasize a commitment to excellence through the provision of affordable, quality and enjoyable
  14|                     For Fall 2010, there were 112 post-graduate and continuing education courses offered, with approximately 875 seats
  15|                     sold. There were 105 courses offered during Spring 2011, with over 930 seats sold.
  16|                     Most recently we have added online courses and online certificate programs to our offerings. This gives individuals
  17|                     the versatility to take a course when it is convenient for them in the comfort of their own home.
  18|                     In August 2010, the program was honored to receive a three-year full accreditation from the Office of Emergency
  19|                     Medical Services for an Emergency Medical Technician (EMT) program, a program that is expected to generate a great deal of interest and address workforce shortages in the region.
  20|                                                                Practical Nursing Program
  21|                     In October 2010, the Practical Nursing Program was reviewed and received full approval status from the Mass Board of
  22|                    Nursing. In May 2011, the program received full accreditation from the Council on Occupational Education. In June
  23|                    2011, thirty-four students graduated from the Practical Nursing Program and entered the nursing profession.
  24|                                          An Experienced Advisory Council -the Monty Tech School Committee
  25|                    The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District Committee is comprised of twenty-two dedicated
  26|                    individuals, whose expertise proves invaluable in advising the district's operations, policies, and procedures.
  27|                     Our students continue to benefit from the broad scope of their experience and varying perspectives, and we are
  28|                    thanktul to the following members of the 2010-2011 School Committee for their outstanding service.

  34|                      4.6.5.1. Purposes. The purposes of this Section are:
  35|                      a) To assure development which is compatible with prevailing architecture and character of the Town.
  36|                      b) To protect the environment.
  37|                      c) To enhance rather than detract from the quality and character of the Town.
  38|                      d) To preserve and enhance property values.
  39|                      4.6.5.2. Standards. In addition to requirements contained elsewhere in this Bylaw the following design elements shall
  40|                      pertain in the Commercial District and, where applicable, shall be reviewed and approved by the Planning Board in
  41|                      connection with Development Plan Review under Section 8.4.
  42|                                                                               82

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                    procedures outlined in Section 8.4., and there shall be sidewalk connections between the buildings. All utilities shall be placed underground.
   2|              3.
   3|              c) Building facades, materials and roof lines shall be consistent with the prevailing architecture and character of the Town and shall be compatible with other structures within the District.
   4|               d) Flat roofs that are visible from the street level are prohibited unless approved by the Planning Board under the
   5|                  Development Plan Review.
   6|               e) Facade and roof colors shall be appropriate to the area and consistent through the site except corporate signs
   7|                  approved under the Development Plan Review.
   8|               f) The principal building(s) shall be connected to public water and sewer where readily available and accessible.
   9|               g) Lighting, signage and architectural style shall be consistent with other uses in the District alternate design, which
  10|                  the Planning Board determines better serves the purposes of this Section is approved by the Planning Board under
  11|                  the Development Plan Review.
  12|               h) LEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design).
  13|                    1. LEED Certification should be encouraged to meet the best practicable level.
  14|               i) Interior Streets, Drives, Walkways and Access.
  15|                     1. Site access shall be a divided way (one way in and one way out).
  16|                     2. Surfaces shall be pervious when possible and practical, shall be of material and appearance consistent
  17|                     throughout the project, and approved by the Planning Board under the Development Plan Review.
  18|               j) Parking and Loading Area.
  19|                     1. Parking shall be in the rear of building(s) and not be visible from the street line wherever possible otherwise
  20|                     approved by the Planning Board under the Development Plan Review.
  21|                     2. All loading docks shall be to the rear of the building(s) and not visible from the street.
  22|                     3. All paved areas shall be separated from the lot line setbacks by a four (4) foot landscaped area of indigenous materials so as to provide a buffer.
  23|               k) Sidewalks.
  24|                     1. Sidewalks shall be provided from the street line, when applicable, and from the parking areas to building(s).
  25|               1) Screening.
  26|                     1. Screening of the site shall be by a four (4) foot landscaped strip at the rear and side lot lines of materials
  27|                     approved by the Planning Board.
  28|                     2. Additional landscaping and screening may be required by the Planning Board during Development Plan Review
  29|                     where the Planning Board deems such additional screening appropriate in order to serve the purposes of this Section.
  30|                m) Landscaping.
  31|                      1. There shall be a minimum of a four (4) foot landscaped area along of the street frontage and along the front and
  32|                      side of the principal building(s) and plantings of indigenous materials along the facades of the building(s) and
  33|                      between the building(s) if there is more than one principal building on site.
  34|                      2. Such landscape shall be a type and height that does not interfere with site lines of drivers.
  35|                      3. Natural features shall be retained to the extent possible.
  36|                      4. A landscape plan shall be provided and approved by the Planning Board under Development Plan Review.
  37|                n) Street Furniture.
  38|                      1. Light fixtures shall be designed to appropriately blend within the district and be of number and height that
  39|                   grants plentiful lighting, but not shine on adjacent properties.
  40|                      2. Lighting must also be placed on the side and rear of the building and shall be approved by the Planning Board,
  41|                   where applicable, under Development Plan Review.
  42|                      3. Outdoor tables, benches, and bicycle racks shall be of a style consistent with the principal use(s) of the site
  43|                      and consistent with the character of the surrounding uses within the District.

  27|               ARTICLE 10. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw Section 3.0. Establishment of Districts, 3.1. by
  28|                adding a new section; "n) Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District" as follows:
  29|               Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District
  30|                PURPOSE
  31|                The Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District has been established to encourage development that in itself is
  32|                compatible and aligns with the character of the Town, created through amenities, a sense of community that is an
  33|                appealing place to live and work ensuring the vision of Lunenburg as a New England Town. Further, by providing clear
  34|                and precise guidelines as to the building and lot design that will revitalize the area and when possible, using
  35|                sustainable building materials and design features that include indoor/outdoor conservation methods.
  36|                LOCATION
  37|                In Lunenburg from the Fitchburg line to the Leominster line including all of Industrial District along Summer Street in
  38|                Lunenburg and all of the Commercial District alon Summer Street and Youngs Road in Lunenburg.
  39|                                                                         89

===PAGE 93=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                        ....... .. |
   2|                 h. Facilitating integrated physical design;
   3|                 i. Promoting a consistent level of design quality;
   4|                j. Encouraging the development of flexible space for small and emerging businesses;
   5|                 K.  Facilitating development proposals responsive to current and future market conditions;
   6|                 1.  Encouraging the development of open spaces and parks within the district to accommodate workers, residents,
   7|                     pedestrians, and shoppers.
   8|                PERMIT GRANTING AUTHORITY
   9|                The Zoning Board of Appeals (ZBA) is hereby the permit granting authority pertaining to the Commercial District and
  10|                where applicable projects proposed within the Overlay District shall be reviewed under Section 8.4 Development Plan
  11|                Review. The Planning Board is hereby the permit granting authority pertaining to the Industrial District where
  12|                applicable projects proposed within the Overlay District shall be reviewed under Section 8.4, Development Plan Review.
  13|                PERMITTED USES
  14|                The following uses are permitted within the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District.
  15|                (a) Any use permitted by right or special permit in the underlying zone.
  16|                (b) Mixed Use Development. - is a development of a tract of land, building, or structure with two (2) or more different
  17|                     uses such as, but not limited to, residential, office, retail, institutional, or entertainment. Residential uses shall be
  18|                     allowed as part of a mixed use project.
  19|                DIMENSIONAL STANDARDS
  20|                These standards herein are hereby established as such to achieve a village style design for the Summer Street
  21|                 Revitalization Overlay District.
  22|                (a) Occupied Lot Area:
  23|                     1. The gross floor area of all buildings and structures on a lot shall not occupy more than forty (40%) percent of
  24|                         the total lot area.
  25|                     2. The total area on any lot devoted to building, parking, outdoor storage, and display and other hard surface
  26|                         areas may occupy up to eighty-five (85%) percent of the total lot area.
  27|                (b) Maximum Height: No more than 55 feet measured from ground level.
  28|                (C) Setbacks:          Front - 20 feet, Side - 15 feet, Rear - 20 feet
  29|                     A landscape plan shall be required for screening and buffering purposes for setback areas.
  30|                (a) Minimum Lot Size: Twenty Thousand (20,000) square feet
  31|                (e) Minimum Frontage: Fifty (50) feet
  32|                (f) Minimum Open Space: All projects within the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District shall have at least ten
  33|                    (10%) of the total site area devoted to Open Space; required setbacks shall be considered as part of the total area
  34|                    required for Open Space. The required Open Space shall not be used for parking or loading purposes and shall be
  35|                     open and unobstructed to the sky, items such as benches, walkways, planters, landscaping, kiosks, gazebos and
  36|                     similar structures shall not be considered obstructions.
  37|                     permitted by the Special Permitting Authority.
  38|                (h) Bonus Density: A Mix Use Development with in the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District which provides
  39|                     at least ten (10%) percent of its residential units be made affordable to low to moderate income persons as defined
  40|                    by the Executive Office of Housing and Economic Development (EOHED).
  41|                ADDTIONAL STANDARDS
  42|                As well as the standards required under Section 8.4 Development Plan Review, these additional standards are
  43|                established for the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District.
  44|                a) Building - Location & Facade

   2|                 m. Allowing a mix of uses in close proximity in the district within the development including residential, retail, office, and light industrial;
   3|                n. Preserving and restoring a village style character to the designated overlay area; o. Promoting a balance of land uses;
   4|                p.
   5|                9. r. Promoting the opportunity for people to work, meet, shop, and utilize services in the vicinity of their residences; Providing opportunities for the development of variety of housing opportunities;
   6|                S.  Providing opportunities for a mixture of uses in the same building;
   7|                t.  Facilitating integrated physical design; Promoting a positive pedestrian environment in the district;
   8|                u.  Promoting a consistent level of design quality;
   9|                V.  Encouraging the development of flexible space for small and emerging businesses;
  10|               X. w. Facilitating development proposals responsive to current and future market conditions;
  11|                   Encouraging the development of open spaces and parks within the district to accommodate workers, residents, pedestrians, and shoppers.

  14|               applicable projects proposed within the Overlay District shall be reviewed under Section 8.4., Development Plan
  15|               Review. The Planning Board is hereby the permit granting authority pertaining to the Industrial District where Review.

  17|              (c) Any use permitted by right or special permit in the underlying zone. The following uses are permitted within the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District.
  18|              (d) Mixed Use Development - is a development of a tract of land, building, or structure with two (2) or more different
  19|              DIMENSIONAL STANDARDS uses such as, but not limited to, residential, office, retail, institutional, or entertainment. Residential uses shall be allowed as part of a mixed use project.
  20|              These standards herein are hereby established as such to achieve a village style design for the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District.
  21|              (i) Occupied Lot Area:
  22|                  3. The total area on any lot devoted to building, parking, outdoor storage, and display and other hard surface areas may occupy up to eighty-five (85%) percent of the total lot area.
  23|             (K) Setbacks: (i) Maximum Height: No more than 55 feet measured from ground level.
  24|                                    Front - 20 feet, Side - 15 feet, Rear - 20 feet
  25|             (1) Minimum Lot Size: Twenty Thousand (20,000) square feet A landscape plan shall be required for screening and buffering purposes for setback areas.
  26|             (m) Minimum Frontage: Fifty (50) feet
  27|             (n) Minimum Open Space: All projects within the Summer Street Revitalization Overlay District shall have at least ten
  28|                 (10%) of the total site area devoted to Open Space; required setbacks shall be considered as part of the total area
  29|                 required for Open Space. The required Open Space shall not be used for parking or loading purposes and shall be
  30|                 open and unobstructed to the sky, items such as benches, walkways, planters, landscaping, kiosks, gazebos and similar structures shall not be considered obstructions.
